## 4. Data formats (normative; byte level; ADR-49)

### 4.1 Conventions

The ES conventions (`faza8-issuer.md:409-433`): big-endian fixed-width integers; `<u16>` and `<u32>` are length-prefixed byte strings; ASCII only where stated; exact lengths, counts within bounds, **no trailing bytes**; lists that are sets are **strictly ascending** in the stated key (one canonical encoding per value); unknown versions refused; `ext` bytes must be 0 in version 1. `day` is `u32` days since 1970-01-01 UTC. A key id is SHA-256 of the 32-byte Ed25519 public key (the Sigsum key hash). An onion is `<u16 ASCII "<56 base32>.onion:<port>">` in its **canonical form only**: lowercase RFC 4648 base32, version byte 3 and SHA3-256 checksum, a decimal port 1 … 65535 without sign, leading zeros or whitespace — the ES parser's rule (`issuer/crates/entitlement/src/onion.rs:1-35`), equivalently `format(parse(s)) = s`; onion lists are ordered and compared by these canonical bytes. The transport's `OnionAddress` (`client-core/net/src/onion.rs:49,62,66`) is lenient — it trims spaces and tabs, lowercases the host and accepts `+80` or `080` as a port — so two spellings of one mirror would pass "distinct onions" and a run's "two mirrors" could collapse into one; it is used only after the canonical check (F-D10). Every parser is total: it never panics and never reads past its input (property tests, fuzzing; §11.1).

### 4.2 Signed statements and signature blocks

```
statement(type, network, body) := "ghost/v1/" ‖ type ‖ 0x00 ‖ u8 network ‖ SHA-256(body)
    type ∈ { "release-root", "release-manifest", "build-attestation" }      (55, 59, 60 bytes)
sigblock := u8 n (1..16) ‖ n × ( key_id[32] ‖ signature[64] )             (key_id strictly ascending)
```

A signature block meets `(keys, t)` when at least `t` entries name a key id in `keys` and verify with `ed25519-dalek` `verify_strict` over the statement; entries naming other keys or failing verification are ignored, never an error (TUF's rule; a block cannot hold two entries for one key, by the ordering rule). The Sigsum leaf statement is `"sigsum.org/v1/tree-leaf" ‖ 0x00 ‖ checksum` (56 bytes; Sigsum log protocol v1), so no GHOST statement and no Sigsum statement can be mistaken for each other.

### 4.3 Root `GHRT`

```
GHRT v1 := body ‖ sigblock over statement("release-root", network, body)
body := "GHRT" ‖ u8 version = 1 ‖ u8 network ‖ u32 root_version (≥ 1)
  ‖ keyset(root)    := u8 n (1..8) ‖ n × pk[32] ‖ u8 t (1..n)          (pk ascending by key id)
  ‖ keyset(release) := u8 n (1..8) ‖ n × pk[32] ‖ u8 t (1..n)
  ‖ keyset(attest)  := u8 n (0..16) ‖ n × pk[32] ‖ u8 t (0..n)
  ‖ apk_signers := u8 n (1..4) ‖ n × cert_sha256[32]                   (ascending)
  ‖ logs := u8 n (1..4) ‖ n × ( pk[32] ‖ <u16 ASCII origin> )          (ascending by key id)
  ‖ witnesses := u8 m (0..32) ‖ m × pk[32] ‖ u8 quorum (0..m)           (ascending by key id)
  ‖ anchor_mirrors := u8 n (2..8) ‖ n × ( onion ‖ operator_id[16] )    (strictly ascending canonical onion)
  ‖ u8 ext = 0
```

Rules: a key appears in at most one keyset of a root; on network 1 (mainnet), `root.t ≥ 2` with at least three root keys, `attest.t ≥ 1`, `quorum ≥ 2`, `m ≥ 3` and anchor mirrors of at least two distinct operator ids; on network 2 (stagenet), `quorum ≥ 1`, `attest.t` may be 0 (staging before an auditor exists; RQ-D15 is a release gate) and the anchor mirrors may share an operator (X-D13); network 3 (regtest) exists only in vectors and test worlds, and production builds refuse it as the ES does. **Anchor mirrors** are the mirrors only the root threshold can change: every update run asks at least one of them (§7.8), so a stolen release key — which can only *add* mirrors through a manifest — can never cut a client off from the vendor's newest root. **Chain rule (TUF §5.3, "new root signed by a threshold of the old and of the new keys"), plus logging:** given trusted `v(n)`, `v(n+1)` is adopted iff same network, `root_version = n + 1`, its signature block meets `(root(n).keys, root(n).t)` **and** `(root(n+1).keys, root(n+1).t)`, **and** a `GHRP` whose message is `SHA-256(v(n+1) file)` verifies under **`v(n)`'s** log policy, with a leaf signed by **a root key or a release key of `v(n)`**. The logging clause closes a targeted root swap: two stolen root keys could otherwise hand a few clients a root naming the attacker's own log and witnesses, after which a forged "logged" manifest would pass; with it, every root change is public in the log the clients already trust before any client adopts it. Allowing a root key to sign the leaf keeps root updates possible when both release tokens are lost or stolen (revision 1 required an old release key, so losing T5 and T6 would have blocked every root update; the root holders sign the 56-byte Sigsum statement on T3, T4 or with `K-ROOT2`; the monitor watches root keys as well as release keys, §5.3). **Trusted root and trust anchor:** a root is trusted if it is the client's trust anchor or reachable from it by the chain rule; the anchor is the newest root embedded in the installed APK's native library (`include_bytes!` of the committed chain, so the anchor is inside the reproducible, logged APK) unless the stored root lies on the same chain, in which case the stored state continues (§7.4 step 0, which also defines the trust epoch). **No root expiry** (D7, §0.4). Residue: a root change needs one log of the previous policy to be alive (L10-D17).

### 4.4 Root chain `GHRC`

```
GHRC v1 := "GHRC" ‖ u8 version = 1 ‖ u8 network ‖ u32 first_version (≥ 1) ‖ u16 count (1..64)
           ‖ count × <u32 GHRT>      (root_version first_version, first_version + 1, …; contiguous)
```

Every client fetches the whole chain (a few KiB a year), so a fetch never reveals which root version the client holds (P-D3). The client verifies from its trusted root upward (§7.4 step 2). Entries at or below its trusted version are compared with its stored chain: a root that differs from the stored one at the same version **and** verifies by the chain rule from its stored predecessor is evidence of root-key equivocation and sets the persistent `ROOT_CONFLICT` flag (revision 1 ignored it silently); an entry that does not verify is ignored. A chain may start above version 1 (revision 1's fixed start and 64-entry cap would have ended the chain's life after 64 root updates): `ghost-release-ops bundle` keeps `first_version` at or below the oldest root embedded in any release at or above the current floor (`anchor_root`, §4.5), so every supported client can walk it; a client whose trusted root is older than `first_version − 1` reports `ROOT_CHAIN_BROKEN` and is below the floor by construction.

### 4.5 Release manifest `GHRM`

```
GHRM v1 := body ‖ sigblock over statement("release-manifest", network, body)     (≤ 65 536 B)
body := "GHRM" ‖ u8 version = 1 ‖ u8 network ‖ u64 manifest_seq (≥ 1)
  ‖ u32 root_version ‖ root_sha256[32]                    (= SHA-256 of that GHRT file)
  ‖ u32 issued_day ‖ u32 not_after_day                     (issued_day < not_after_day ≤ issued_day + 91)
  ‖ u32 floor_version_code                                  (minimum supported; 0 = none; ≤ 2^31 − 1 and ≤ the
                                                              highest attested flavor-1 version_code below, §7.4 step 3)
  ‖ u8 nrel (1..4)    ‖ nrel × release                      (strictly ascending version_code; one min_sdk for all)
  ‖ u8 nmirror (0..16) ‖ nmirror × ( onion ‖ operator_id[16] )   (extra mirrors only: strictly ascending canonical
                                                              onion, none of them an anchor mirror of the root)
  ‖ u16 nrelay (0..512) ‖ nrelay × ( onion ‖ operator_id[16] ‖ u8 flags = 0 )   (strictly ascending canonical onion;
                                                              an operator id never changes for a known onion, §7.8)
  ‖ u16 nbridge = 0                                         (reserved for ADR-16, Phase 12)
  ‖ u8 ext = 0
release := u32 version_code (1 … 2^31 − 1) ‖ <u16 ASCII version_name (1..32)> ‖ u8 min_sdk ‖ u8 flavor (1 = mirror)
  ‖ u32 anchor_root                                         (the newest root embedded in this build)
  ‖ u8 release_flags                                        (bit 0 = wire-changing, P-D5; others 0)
  ‖ apk_sha256[32] ‖ u64 apk_length (1 … 268 435 456)
  ‖ unsigned_sha256[32] ‖ u64 unsigned_length
  ‖ u16 rollout_permille (0..1000) ‖ rollout_salt[16]      (the salt fixed for the release's life, §7.6)
  ‖ commit[20]                                              (git SHA-1 of the tagged commit)
  ‖ u64 es_seq ‖ es_sha256[32]                              (the ES embedded in this build)
  ‖ u8 nart (1..32) ‖ nart × ( u8 kind ‖ sha256[32] ‖ u64 length )   (strictly ascending kind; kind 16 required)
  ‖ sigblock_or_empty over statement("build-attestation", network, GHRA(release))   (u8 n may be 0)
```

Artifact kinds (§6.1): 1, 2 native library arm64-v8a, x86_64; 3, 4 their symbol files; 5 `ghost-relay`; 6 `ghost-issuer`; 7 `ghost-issuer-ops`; 8 `ghost-release-ops`; 9 the APK notices asset; 10, 11 relay and issuer notices; 12, 13, 14 client, relay and issuer SBOMs; 15 the MPL source bundle; 16 the source-escrow digest (length = escrow archive length; **required** in every release, so the attested source is bound by SHA-256 and not only by the git commit's SHA-1); 17 the ES file; 18 the builder recipe (Dockerfile ‖ `pins.txt`); 19 the exported public source tree (`ghost-verify` and the public kit, D21); 20, 21, 22 `ghost-verify` for x86_64 Linux, aarch64 Linux and x86_64 Windows; 23 the per-version evidence of ADR-38 point 8 (RQ-D32; not in `SHA256SUMS`, since CI gate reports are not build outputs); 24 the wire transcript (G-D11). The manifest carries no time finer than a day and nothing per client. Every release of one manifest has the same `min_sdk` (a choice among releases by SDK would tell the mirror the device's Android range); a device below it reports `SDK_UNSUPPORTED` and fetches no APK.

### 4.6 Attestation statement `GHRA` (what an independent builder signs)

```
GHRA v1 := "GHRA" ‖ u8 version = 1 ‖ u8 network ‖ commit[20] ‖ u32 version_code ‖ u8 flavor ‖ u32 anchor_root
  ‖ unsigned_sha256[32] ‖ u64 unsigned_length ‖ u64 es_seq ‖ es_sha256[32]
  ‖ u8 nart ‖ nart × ( u8 kind ‖ sha256[32] ‖ u64 length )       (every kind of the release except 23)
```

The client rebuilds `GHRA` from the release record's own fields; an attester never sees or signs the signed APK. The human-readable attestation (who built, where, with which recipe, what was reviewed, and the signing-certificate digest it expects, which makes it the second independent place for the fingerprint, §5.4) is a separate document in the archive (§5.5).

### 4.7 Log proof `GHRP` (the Sigsum v1 components in a GHOST container)

```
GHRP v1 := "GHRP" ‖ u8 version = 1 ‖ message[32]          (= SHA-256 of the logged GHRM or GHRT file; the leaf checksum is SHA-256(message))
  ‖ u8 log (index into the governing root's logs: the manifest's root, or for a root v(n+1), v(n))
  ‖ leaf_signature[64] ‖ leaf_key_id[32]                   (over the Sigsum leaf statement: for a manifest, a release key of
                                                              its root; for a root v(n+1), a root or release key of v(n))
  ‖ u64 tree_size ‖ u64 leaf_index (< tree_size) ‖ u8 npath (0..63) ‖ npath × node[32]
  ‖ root_hash[32] ‖ log_signature[64]                     (the log's signature on its tree head)
  ‖ u8 ncos (0..32) ‖ ncos × ( witness_key_id[32] ‖ u64 time ‖ cosignature[64] )   (ascending key id)
```

The leaf is `checksum ‖ leaf_signature ‖ leaf_key_id` (128 bytes) and its tree hash `SHA-256(0x00 ‖ leaf)` (RFC 6962 §2, as Sigsum v1 states); the inclusion path is checked by the RFC 6962 / RFC 9162 algorithm; the tree head is the C2SP checkpoint body with origin `sigsum.org/v1/tree/<hex key hash of the log>` signed by the log; each cosignature is a C2SP `cosignature/v1` signature over `"cosignature/v1\ntime <t>\n" ‖ checkpoint body`. Sigsum's own proof-bundle and policy formats are "work in progress" (sigsum.org/docs), so GHOST carries the components itself; **the exact checkpoint bytes are fixed by vectors produced with sigsum-go v0.14.1 and checked by GHOST's parser (V-D11a)**, not by this paragraph. Cosignature times may not exceed the device's trusted time by more than **σ + 1 day = 4 days** (σ = 3 days, F7:58: a device that reached READY can be that far from true time, and revision 1's one day refused every fresh proof on a device two or three days slow); there is **no lower bound**: an old cosigned checkpoint still proves that the manifest was logged. The upper bound is a sanity check, not a freshness mechanism (freshness is the manifest's expiry).

### 4.8 Bundle `GHRB` (what a mirror serves) and bounds

```
GHRB v1 := "GHRB" ‖ u8 version = 1 ‖ u8 network ‖ <u32 GHRC>
  ‖ u16 nroot ‖ nroot × <u32 GHRP>        (one proof per chain entry after the first, ascending: the root-logging clause of §4.3)
  ‖ <u32 GHRM> ‖ <u32 GHRP>               (the manifest and its proof)                                   (≤ 1 048 576 B)
```

Bounds, fixed before reading: bundle 1 MiB; root 16 KiB; manifest 64 KiB; proof 16 KiB; APK exactly `apk_length` (≤ 256 MiB). Worst cases from the formats (recomputed for revision 2): a root ≈ 4.8 KiB (16 attestation keys, 32 witnesses, 4 logs, 8 anchor mirrors, a full signature block), a proof ≈ 5.5 KiB (63 path nodes, 32 cosignatures), a manifest ≈ 58.3 KiB at every maximum at once (4 releases × 32 artifacts × 16 attestations, 16 extra mirrors, 512 relays, 16 signatures), so 64 roots with their proofs and one manifest (≈ 714 KiB) fit in 1 MiB; a realistic bundle (a few roots, tens of relays) is tens of KiB. A response longer than its bound is cut at the bound and refused (endless-data attack, TUF); a shorter one is refused.

### 4.9 Files in the repository

`ghost/protocol/release/README.md` (this section, condensed); `ghost/protocol/release/<network>/root-<n>.ghrt` (the committed chain, the anchor's source); `…/apk-signer.crt` (after an RK3, also the new certificate); `…/anchor-mirrors.txt` (`mirror <onion:port> <operator id hex>`: the input of `root-build`; the client reads the anchor mirrors from the root only); `ghost/protocol/release/<network>/allowed_signers` (git tag keys); `ghost/protocol/test-vectors/release_{root,manifest,proof,bundle,apk_core,onion}.txt` with every negative case of §11.1, and `ghost/protocol/test-vectors/apk/` (crafted APKs generated once with apksigner and throwaway keys, committed with those keys; a G-D1 fixture root); `ghost/protocol/test-vectors/wire_transcript.txt` (G-D11). The existing `ghost/protocol/entitlement/relay-directory.txt` stays the bootstrap relay list.

---

## 5. Transparency log, attestations, publication (normative; ADR-49)

### 5.1 Why Sigsum, and the policy

Sigsum's log server protocol is a stable v1 (v1.0.1); its witness cosigning protocol is a release candidate; its proof-bundle and trust-policy formats are work in progress (sigsum.org/docs). A leaf is `checksum[32] ‖ signature[64] ‖ key_hash[32]`; logs accept only 32-byte messages and store nothing else (the log protocol, `git.glasklar.is/sigsum/…/log.md`). Glasklar Teknik's named production policy `sigsum-generic-2025-1` lists two logs (Glasklar Teknik, Mullvad VPN) and three witnesses (Glasklar Teknik, Mullvad VPN, Tillitis), and itself names the weakness that the three witness operators share a parent company (glasklarteknik.se, 2026-02-04). GHOST encodes that policy's logs, witnesses and quorum in the root (`GHRT.logs`, `GHRT.witnesses`, `quorum`), **transcribed from the policy file in S0a (V-D11a, which needs no domain)**; the flat k-of-m quorum must be at least as strict as the policy's own rule. Rekor v2 (GA 2025-10-10) is rejected for now: at GA its post says witnessing "will be implemented soon", and its log shards rotate yearly. A GHOST-run log is rejected: its operator is the vendor (AD-V), and independent witnesses would have to agree to watch it. (Precedent for logging release metadata rather than binaries: Google's Pixel Binary Transparency log of factory-image metadata.)

**What is logged.** One leaf per manifest (`message = SHA-256(GHRM file)`, signed by `K-REL1` or `K-REL2`) and one leaf per new root version (`message = SHA-256(GHRT file)`, logged under the previous root's policy and signed by a root or release key of the previous root, §4.3), in Sigsum's namespace; the log stores `checksum = SHA-256(message)`, so a monitor matches a leaf to a file by `SHA-256(SHA-256(file))`. A manifest commits to its root (`root_sha256`), to every release's signed APK, unsigned core, artifacts, commit and ES, and to the attestations, so one leaf logs the whole release state; relay and issuer binaries, symbols, SBOMs and the escrow digest are logged through it.

**Submission and rate limiting.** Public Sigsum logs are expected to apply domain-based rate limiting: a `sigsum-token` header signed by a key whose public key sits in a DNS TXT record `_sigsum_v1.<domain>` the submitter controls (the `sigsum-tools(7)` manual: "as publicly accessible logs are expected to do"; log.md). So publishing needs a DNS domain (Q4, OA-4), used only for that record (and, if ever, Accrescent's check, §8.2); **no client ever contacts it** (LIM:158 stands for the app). The leaf is signed offline by the token; `ghost-release-ops leaf-request` writes the body of a Sigsum add-leaf request (`sigsum-submit` without `-k` "must be the body of an add-leaf request … parsed and verified before submission"); the owner submits it **over Tor** with sigsum-go's `sigsum-submit -p <policy> -a <K-RL> -d <domain>` (v0.14.1, BSD-2-Clause; `-a` takes an OpenSSH-format key), which collects the inclusion proof and the cosigned tree head that `ghost-release-ops proof-assemble` turns into a `GHRP`. **How "over Tor" is made true (normative):** the publication steps of §5.2 run inside the builder image (Linux, so the Windows workstation needs no Linux tools of its own) in a container attached only to an internal Docker network whose one other member is a Tor client container; `sigsum-submit` gets `HTTPS_PROXY=socks5://tor:9050`, which Go's default HTTP transport honours (sigsum-go builds a plain `http.Client{}`), and the container has no other route, so a tool that ignored the proxy would fail rather than leak. `torsocks` is never used for Go tools: Go issues raw system calls, and the torsocks README states it is then "useless" and traffic leaves outside Tor. V-D11b records a packet capture of a submission showing no packet outside the Tor container's connections.

**Staging.** The stagenet chain may use the public logs (real path, cheap). Until the domain exists (OA-4), the JVM and Rust worlds use generated log and witness keys, and the live drill waits (§13.3 item 8). The vectors and the policy's exact bytes need no domain (V-D11a, S0a); only the submission does (V-D11b, S0b).

### 5.2 Publication procedure (RK1 continued, online; runbook R-P)

All steps run in the Tor-only publication container described above (runbook R-P).

1. Bring back from the signer host: signed APK, `GHRM`, the add-leaf request (and, after an RK2, the new root's request).
2. `ghost-release-ops apk-core --apk <signed> --expect <unsigned sha256>` again (the online re-check of `core(signed) = unsigned`).
3. `sigsum-submit` through the container's Tor proxy; `proof-assemble` → `GHRP`; `bundle` → `GHRB` (with the committed `GHRC` and the roots' proofs), the regenerated `archive-index.txt` and the browser listener's static index page (§7.1).
4. `ghost-verify apk --bundle current.ghrb --apk <signed> --root <anchor> --sdk 29` and `--sdk 33` must each print `VERIFIED` exactly as a client at that SDK would decide (§7.4), including the attestation clause.
5. Upload over each mirror's upload onion (SSH with client authorisation, a Tor instance separate from the serving one, §7.1): archive files first, then `current.ghrb` by atomic rename.
6. Fetch `current.ghrb` and the APK back from every mirror through Tor, over both listeners, and compare hashes; record the run in the release record.

### 5.3 The release record and monitors

**Release record** (the mirror's `/ghost/v1/<network>/archive/`, every file named by its SHA-256): every `GHRM` and `GHRP` ever logged, every `GHRT`, every signed APK, the unsigned core's hash list from both environments (`SHA256SUMS-A` and `SHA256SUMS-B` as the jobs printed them — **no CI run links**: the repository is private, so a link verifies nothing for the public and names the owner's account), the symbol files, SBOMs, notices, the MPL source bundle (ADR-37 point 2), the ES file of each release, `ghost-relay` (operators; ADR-37 point 6), `ghost-verify` with the exported public source, the human-readable attestations, the escrow digest, the per-version evidence of ADR-38 point 8 (artifact kind 23: the threat model, the guarantee matrix, the residual limits and the gate results of the tagged commit, RQ-D32), `archive-index.txt` (one line per logged manifest: pair, SHA-256, log, leaf index, releases with their `apk_sha256` and salt), and the RK1 checklist (redacted of PINs and serial numbers). Every file passes G-D9's identity scrub before upload. The `ghost-issuer` binary is logged and attested but not published (owner-operated).

**Monitor** (`ghost-verify monitor`, in the public repository; anyone can run it). **Who:** an independent party — the auditor of OA-9 under LG-6 — runs it at least weekly and before every release gate, and publishes signed results (a release gate, §13.3); the owner runs it too. An owner-run monitor alone cannot detect the owner. **Rules**, for every leaf of the policy logs whose key hash is a release key **or a root key** of any GHOST root: (a) the archive holds a manifest or a root whose `SHA-256(SHA-256(file))` is the leaf's checksum (the log stores the checksum of the message, and the message is the file's SHA-256) and whose signatures verify (a root also by the chain rule from its predecessor); (b) no two manifests share a `(root_version, manifest_seq)` pair, and within each log pairs increase with the leaf index (no order is defined across the two logs; a pair logged in both must be the same manifest); (c) every release's APK is in the archive with the stated hash, its core (§6.5) matches the attested hash and every attestation verifies; (d) the ES of consecutive releases is append-only (`ghost-issuer-ops schedule-verify --previous`, ADR-22 rule 5), which closes F8 F6's "key consistency against a malicious release key" for every logged release; (e) each symbol file's build-id equals its library's; (f) **what was served**: the monitor fetches `current.ghrb` from every listed mirror at random times, at least daily, and records each pair seen and for how long; a logged manifest never observed served, or observed for less than 24 hours, or whose archive copy appeared more than 7 days after its leaf, is flagged — a vendor that logs a manifest and serves it only to a target for a short window is still logged, but no longer silently; (g) each release's salt is unchanged across re-signings and unique across releases; (h) the floor of every manifest respects §7.4 step 3, `issued_day` never decreases with the sequence, and a release flagged wire-changing never sits strictly between 0 and 1000 permille; (i) a leaf signed by a drill root's key is reported as a drill, never as a release. A failure is a public alarm (RK4). The claim this supports is "**every manifest any updater accepts is in the public log and archive**", not "every user sees the same manifest at the same time".

### 5.4 First installs, operators and the public repository

- **`ghost-verify`** (Rust, the `ghost-client-update` format crate plus a CLI; in the separate public repository with a permissive licence, ADR-38 point 7, Q28): `apk --bundle --apk [--root-sha256] [--sdk <n>]` performs §7.4's decision offline (for each SDK class: 29–32 and 33+, unless `--sdk` names one) and prints a fixed vocabulary (`VERIFIED version=… version_code=… manifest_seq=… log=<origin> cosigned=<k>/<m> attested=<k>/<t> signer=<sha256> sdk=<n>` or `REFUSED <code>`); `libs --apk` lists the APK's native libraries with their hashes against the release's artifacts (the black-box kit's native-library check, ADR-38 point 7); `fetch --socks <127.0.0.1:9050|9150>` fetches the bundle and an APK from a mirror's HTTP/1.1 listener over a local Tor (optional, compiled separately); `operator --binary <file> --kind relay|issuer` checks an operator binary against the newest verified manifest; `monitor` as §5.3. **Platforms** (Q41): static x86_64 and aarch64 Linux binaries and an x86_64 Windows binary, built by the release recipe from the exported public source (artifact kinds 19–22) and published in the public repository and in the mirrors' archive; macOS users build from source. Its own builds are reproducible from the public source, which is the point: the verifier is the one GHOST component users can read.
- **Manual path** (weaker, no log check): the signing certificate's SHA-256, checkable with `apksigner verify --print-certs` (Signal's documented practice) or with AppVerifier (ISC; it compares package name and certificate hash), published in **two independent places** — the public repository's README (under the project identity, reachable over Tor, Q28) and the auditor's published attestation, which states the digest it expects (§4.6) — and, as a convenience, on the mirrors' index page, which is the channel being verified and so not independent. Revision 1's third place, a signed tag message, is not public: tags live in the private repository and carry the tagger's name, email and time zone.
- **Operators** check `ghost-relay` with `ghost-verify operator` before every start of a new version; the relay image (`infra/relay/Dockerfile.release`, S8) copies the released binary after `sha256sum -c` against the manifest's artifact list, instead of compiling the source (the compose file of `infra/relay/docker-compose.staging.yml` builds from source today, which closed-source operators cannot do). The issuer image follows the same pattern (owner-operated; consistency with the log).

### 5.5 Attestations (T12b mechanics)

An independent builder (Phase 15's auditor or later ones) receives the tagged commit (source access under ADR-38 point 4), builds it with the committed recipe (§6) on its own machine, compares its `SHA256SUMS` with the vendor's, and if equal signs `GHRA` with its key (listed in the root by RK2) and publishes a human-readable attestation (identity of the attester, date, recipe digest, hashes, the signing-certificate digest, what was reviewed). RK1 embeds the signatures in the release record. With `attest.t = 0` (stagenet before an auditor) the clause is vacuous, and the UI may not claim reproduction (§2.6); it also leaves the floor bound of §7.4 step 3 to any listed release, which is why the floor is scoped to its root version. On mainnet `attest.t ≥ 1` (§4.3), so **no mainnet release can reach an updater without an auditor's attestation** — an availability dependency on the auditor, accepted (R-D10; mitigated by a second attester, never by lowering the threshold).

---

## 6. Reproducible build from two environments (normative; ADR-50)

### 6.1 What "identical hash" means

The release outputs of commit C are, in a fixed order with fixed names, the files of `SHA256SUMS` (LF, sorted by path, `sha256␠␠path`):

| Path | What | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `apk/ghost-<network>-<version_code>-unsigned.apk` | the **zip-aligned unsigned APK**: AGP's `assembleRelease` output if `zipalign -c -P 16 -v 4` passes on it, else `zipalign -P 16 -f 4` of it (V-D4 decides once; both environments run the same script) | the bytes `core(signed)` must equal (§6.5) |
| `lib/<abi>/libghost_client_net.so` (arm64-v8a, x86_64) | built with the `client-release` profile, stripped after link, with a SHA-1 GNU build-id | what the APK contains (`apk-native-libs.sh`) |
| `symbols/<abi>/libghost_client_net.so.debug` | split line-table debug info | ADR-38 point 9 |
| `bin/ghost-relay`, `bin/ghost-issuer`, `bin/ghost-issuer-ops`, `bin/ghost-release-ops` | `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, `--release --locked`, `[profile.release]` (stripped, as today), **each in its own `cargo build` invocation** (Cargo unifies features across the packages of one invocation, so a joint build would differ from the `-p ghost-relay-node` build operators, the relay Dockerfile, the auditor and `rust-feature-policy.sh` see) | relay and issuer: ADR-38 point 5; release-ops: it runs on the signer host |
| `bin/ghost-verify-{x86_64-linux,aarch64-linux,x86_64-windows}` and `src/ghost-public-<tag>.tar` | `ghost-verify` built from the public tree `ghost/public/` (static musl for Linux, `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu` with Debian's mingw-w64), and that tree exported as a deterministic tar (sorted entries, fixed owner and mode, `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` times) — what the new public repository receives (D21, Q41) | artifact kinds 19–22; V-D25 |
| `notices/THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES-{android,client-net,relay,issuer}.txt` | regenerated, equal to the committed files (G-L1) | ADR-37 point 3 |
| `sbom/{client,relay,issuer}.cdx.json` | CycloneDX 1.7 (released 2025-10-21; ECMA-424 2nd ed.), generated by `ghost-release-ops sbom` from `cargo metadata --locked` per shipped target and from `:app:releaseRuntimeComponents` (`app/build.gradle.kts:183-197`) with `verification-metadata.xml` digests; sorted keys and arrays, **no timestamp**, no serial number | ADR-37 point 8, C-08 |
| `recipe/Dockerfile`, `recipe/pins.txt` | the recipe itself | artifact kind 18 |

The **signed APK is not** in `SHA256SUMS`: its hash differs by the signing block; the manifest binds both (`apk_sha256`, `unsigned_sha256`). Today's `reproducible-build.sh` proves same-path determinism of the unsigned APK (`reproducible-build.sh:30-41`) and `android-native` of the libraries (`ci.yml:219-232`); both stay, and the cross-environment gate is new.

### 6.2 The builder recipe (`ghost/infra/builder/`)

`Dockerfile`, `x86_64` only, every input pinned, nothing fetched unpinned:

| Input | Pin | Today |
|---|---|---|
| base image | `debian:bookworm-slim@sha256:<digest>` (Q12: trixie acceptable; bookworm matches the relay and issuer images) | relay: `rust:1.98.1-bookworm` (`infra/relay/Dockerfile:5`) |
| apt packages | `snapshot.debian.org` at one timestamp, `check-valid-until=no`, exact versions | — |
| JDK | Temurin 17, one exact `17.0.x+y` tarball by SHA-256 (V-D17) | CI: `temurin` `"17"` with the patch unpinned (`ci.yml:306,465`) |
| Android SDK | each package archive (`platforms;android-37.0`, `platforms;android-37.2`, `build-tools;37.0.0`, `build-tools;36.0.0`, `ndk;30.0.16248370`) fetched directly from its repository URL and checked against the **SHA-256 recorded in `pins.txt`** before unpacking; `sdkmanager` is not used in the builder (it verifies against Google's live repository manifest, not against a pin) | `ci.yml:43-44`; `app/build.gradle.kts:62` |
| Rust | the 1.98.1 standalone installer archives (`rust-1.98.1-x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, `rust-std-1.98.1-<target>` for `aarch64-linux-android`, `x86_64-linux-android`, `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, `x86_64-unknown-linux-musl`, `aarch64-unknown-linux-musl`, `x86_64-pc-windows-gnu`) by the SHA-256 in `pins.txt`; `rustup` is not used in the builder (it resolves the live channel manifest) | `rust-toolchain.toml` |
| cargo-ndk | 4.1.2, `cargo install --locked` | `build-native.sh:16` |
| protoc | 36.1 by SHA-256 | `install-protoc.sh:6-7` |
| Gradle | 9.7.1 wrapper by `distributionSha256Sum` | `gradle-wrapper.properties:3-4` |
| Gradle and Maven dependencies | `verification-metadata.xml` checksums, **aapt2 jars pinned** (today trusted without checksum, `verification-metadata.xml:8`) | 332 components pinned |
| Cargo dependencies | `Cargo.lock` checksums, crates.io only (`deny.toml`) | — |

**`scripts/release/build-release.sh <tag>`** (the same script in A, B and C): (1) `git clone` of the repository into `/w/src` on a tmpfs (sources only; git writes them in index order) and `git checkout --detach <tag>`, `git verify-tag` when the allowed-signers file is present; build directories (`CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/target`, Gradle's `build/` and caches) stay on the container's disk, since an 8 GB runner cannot also hold them, the Gradle and Kotlin daemons and R8 in RAM; (2) fixed environment (§6.3), including the processor pin N = 2; (3) `build-native.sh` with the `client-release` profile and the symbol split (§6.3) into `/w/out/lib` and `/w/out/symbols`, copied into `android/network/src/main/jniLibs`; (4) `cargo build --release --locked --target x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu -p <package>` once per binary (`ghost-relay-node`, `ghost-issuer`, `ghost-issuer-ops`, `ghost-release-ops`), then `ghost-verify` for its three targets from `ghost/public/`, with the same remaps; (5) `./gradlew --no-daemon --no-build-cache :app:assembleRelease` with `org.gradle.workers.max=2`, then the alignment step of §6.1; (6) `license-notices.sh` and `apk-notices.sh` (G-L1, G-L3), `apk-native-libs.sh`; (7) `ghost-release-ops sbom` and the public-tree export; (8) G-D9's identity scrub over `/w/out`; (9) `SHA256SUMS` over `/w/out`. Network use is limited to fetching pinned inputs; a step that downloads anything not pinned fails G-D2's static check of the script.

`pins.txt` is the single source of every version and digest, the SDK, NDK and Rust archives' SHA-256 included; gate **G-D2** fails when `ci.yml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, `gradle-wrapper.properties`, `install-protoc.sh`, `build-native.sh` or the Dockerfile disagree with it, or when any build file sets `target-cpu=native`, `-march=native` or `-mtune=native`. The image is built from the Dockerfile in each environment (G-L5 forbids pushing images, `no-public-binaries.sh:9-11`); image digests are recorded, not required to match (making the image itself reproducible with BuildKit's `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` is a stretch goal, not a gate).

### 6.3 Determinism measures and the known hazards

| Hazard (source) | Measure | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Build paths in Rust output: Windows `\` separators, remap flags hashed into build-script directory names (`build-native.sh:9-11`; F9 hygiene §10's note on `relay/crates/api/build.rs`) | Linux container; the checkout at `/w/src`, `CARGO_HOME=/cargo`, `CARGO_TARGET_DIR=/target` in every environment; the existing remaps and leak check kept | measured cross-env in V-D2 |
| File-system ordering (tools that do not sort directory listings) | the **sources** are cloned into a **tmpfs** inside the container, so their listing order follows git's creation order, identical everywhere (ext4's hashed directories order by a per-file-system seed); tmpfs does **not** order outputs that parallel workers create, so build directories stay on disk and any tool whose output depends on listing order is found by the variation matrix (sources tmpfs vs overlay; A vs B) and fixed at the tool | V-D3 |
| CPU-count-dependent output (R8, F-Droid's list; a 2026 R8 regression reported in Google issue 554586985, details behind a login) | **no `docker run --cpus`** (Docker refuses a value above the host's CPU count, moby issue 40303, and B has 2 CPUs, so `--cpus 4` would fail job B outright); instead the processor count every tool sees is pinned to N = 2 whatever the host: `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS=-XX:ActiveProcessorCount=2` (every JVM: Gradle, the Kotlin daemon, R8's workers), `org.gradle.workers.max=2`, `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS=2`; Gradle and Kotlin heaps fixed for an 8 GB host | V-D3 (N = 2 vs 8 through `ActiveProcessorCount`), V-D10, V-D16 |
| Host CPU model and kernel (A: the owner's laptop CPU under WSL2; B: an Azure VM) | no `target-cpu=native`, `-march=native` or `-mtune=native` anywhere (G-D2); only an A-vs-B run can reveal a remaining dependence, which the variation matrix cannot vary | V-D2 |
| Clock, locale, time zone, umask, user | `TZ=UTC`, `LANG=C.UTF-8`, `umask 022`, uid 1000, `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` = the commit time (for any tool that honours it); builds on two different days | the same-path T12 builds minutes apart already show no clock input (`reproducible-build.sh:30-41`, green on `e267b3f`, STATUS:17); V-D3 adds days |
| `META-INF/version-control-info.textproto` (AGP ≥ 8.3, on by default; F-Droid's list) | `vcsInfo { include = false }` in the release build type (D12) | V-D4 |
| Dependency metadata block | already off (`app/build.gradle.kts:84-89`) | — |
| AGP re-stripping the native library | already off (`app/build.gradle.kts:81`) | — |
| Symbols and build-id | a dedicated `[profile.client-release]` (`inherits = "release"`, `debug = "line-tables-only"`, `strip = "none"`) used only by `build-native.sh`, with `-C link-arg=-Wl,--build-id=sha1`; after linking, NDK `llvm-objcopy --only-keep-debug` then `--strip-all` (`.dynsym` stays, so G-R6 keeps working). `[profile.release]` (`ghost/Cargo.toml:97-99`) is unchanged: it serves every binary of the workspace, so changing it would ship unstripped `ghost-relay` and `ghost-issuer` to operators and add debug info to every CI release build (the T2 and DM gates, `ghost-dm-host`) | V-D9 |
| libcrux SIMD build scripts without `rerun-if-env-changed` | the existing clean before every client build (`build-native.sh:59-66`), G-R3 | unchanged |
| JDK patch level | pinned (V-D17) | — |
| aapt2 | pinned jar checksums; its output deterministic given the jar | V-D2 |
| Bundled C code (`libsqlite3-sys` "bundled", `client-core/net/Cargo.toml`) | NDK clang pinned; `.comment` strings are a drift detector | V-D2 |
| Third-party prebuilt `.so` (`libsqlcipher.so`, `libandroidx.graphics.path.so`) | compared by name only today (`apk-native-libs.sh:5-7`); their bytes are fixed by the pinned AAR and the pinned NDK strip; declared, not built from source | declared (L10-D7) |

### 6.4 The two environments and the comparison

- **Environment A — the owner's workstation**: Docker Desktop on the owner's Windows machine (not the `ghost-runner` distribution, whose Docker Engine the CI jobs control, `ci-self-hosted.md:34`), `scripts/release/build-release.sh <tag>` inside the image. Its outputs are the ones carried to the signer host. Both engines run in the same WSL2 virtual machine, which is why A's outputs are trusted only when they equal B's.
- **Environment B — a GitHub-hosted runner**: `ubuntu-24.04`, which for a private repository is 2 CPUs, 8 GB RAM and 14 GB SSD (docs.github.com, "GitHub-hosted runners"); the same script in the same image built there (rebuilt from scratch each run: snapshot packages, SDK, NDK, Rust, Gradle and Cargo dependencies — an estimated 60–120 minutes per run on 2 vCPUs, which V-D10 measures); a cleanup step frees disk first; its `SHA256SUMS` reaches the comparison as a job output (text, G-L5), and the job prints the first 16 hex digits of the file's SHA-256 in its summary for the owner's independent-device check (RK1 step 2). The private plan includes 2 000 hosted minutes a month (`ci-self-hosted.md:3`), blocked at exhaustion without a payment method (ADR-39 point 3): the budget of §12.4 (Q42) plans them. The fee GitHub announced for self-hosted runner minutes from 2026-03-01 was **postponed** ("We're postponing the announced billing change for self-hosted GitHub Actions", GitHub changelog 2025-12-16, updated), contrary to one research digest. If V-D10 shows the hosted runner cannot fit the build, the fallback is a second machine of the owner's that never runs the CI runners (Q11).
- **Why not the self-hosted runner as B**: it is the same laptop and the same WSL2 VM as A (`ci-self-hosted.md` §7, "Toate distribuțiile WSL2 … rulează în aceeași mașină virtuală"), so a compromise of one is likely a compromise of both.
- **Comparison** (`release-build.yml`, on a signed tag and by dispatch, and on `main` only when the recipe, `pins.txt`, a lock file or `verification-metadata.xml` changes, plus once a month to catch drift — not weekly, which would spend 5–9 hosted hours a month on its own): job A on the self-hosted runner, job B hosted, job C diffs the two `SHA256SUMS` and checks completeness against the expected path list (G-D3). **Diagnosing a difference needs B's bytes**, and the artifact quota has been exhausted since 2026-09-20 (STATUS:24): on a mismatch job B saves its outputs to the run's Actions cache under `ghost-release-diag-<run_id>`, a new G-L5 hand-off name (private repository only, restored by job C of the same run), and job C, on the self-hosted runner, restores them and runs diffoscope against A's. The owner's local environment-A run for a release repeats job A's script; RK1 requires `local A = job A = job B`.
- **Environment C — the auditor** (release gate): the same recipe on the auditor's machine, signed `GHRA` (§5.5).

### 6.5 Signing preserves the core

The v2/v3 signing block is inserted immediately before the ZIP central directory (source.android.com, v2), and the end-of-central-directory record's offset field points at the moved central directory. **apksigner also writes zero padding before the block**, so that the block starts on a 4 096-byte boundary: apksig's `ApkSigner` (step 11) consumes `ByteBuffer.allocate(padding)` before the block, `DefaultApkSignerEngine.outputZipSections2` passes `apkSigningBlockPaddingSupported = true`, and `ApkSigningBlockUtils.generateApkSigningBlockPadding` pads to `ANDROID_COMMON_PAGE_ALIGNMENT_BYTES = 4096`, with no option that disables it (apksigcopier's README likewise inserts the block "adding zero padding if needed"). **Measured** with build-tools 37.0.0 (Appendix C.3): an unsigned APK of 5 248 bytes with its central directory at 5 100, signed with `--alignment-preserved true`, v1 and v4 off and `--min-sdk-version 29`, gives 3 092 zero bytes at 5 100, the block at 8 192 (`s` = 4 088) and the central directory at 12 288. Revision 1's statement "the signed file is the unsigned file plus the block" was therefore false, and its core (the file minus the block) would have been 8 340 bytes against an unsigned APK of 5 248: every real release would have failed `APK_CORE`.

**The core rule** (normative; the same code in `ghost-release-ops apk-core`, the client's step 9 and `ghost-verify`). With `length`, `cd_offset` and `cd_size` from the signed file's end-of-central-directory record, `s` from the block's footer and `unsigned_length` from the release:

1. `cd_offset + cd_size = length − 22` (the central directory runs to the end record, comment length 0);
2. `E := unsigned_length − cd_size − 22` (where the unsigned APK's central directory began) and `B := cd_offset − s − 8` (the block's start) satisfy `0 < E ≤ B` and `B = E + ((4096 − E mod 4096) mod 4096)`;
3. every byte of `[E, B)` is zero;
4. **core** := `file[0, E) ‖ file[cd_offset, length)`, with the end record's central-directory offset field set to `E`.

Measured on the same files, this core equals the unsigned APK byte for byte (identical SHA-256). The padding is fully determined (its length by `E`, its bytes zero), so nothing Android would read can hide in it; the block itself stays outside the core as before. build-tools ≥ 35 `apksigner` also re-aligns entries by default ("forcibly replaces existing alignment padding", apksigcopier README; F-Droid fdroiddata issue 3299); build-tools 37.0.0's own help says `--alignment-preserved` "Whether the existing alignment within the APK should be preserved; the default for this setting is false", with `--lib-page-alignment` defaulting to 16 384 (read locally, 2026-10-03). RK1 therefore signs with:

```
apksigner sign --provider-class sun.security.pkcs11.SunPKCS11 --provider-arg pkcs11.cfg \
  --ks NONE --ks-type PKCS11 --ks-key-alias <alias> \
  --v1-signing-enabled false --v2-signing-enabled true --v3-signing-enabled true \
  --v4-signing-enabled false --alignment-preserved true --min-sdk-version 29 \
  --out signed.apk unsigned.apk
```

**After an RK3** (rotation targeted at API 33, the default), every release is signed in one run by the **original and the new key**: the original signer, then `--next-signer` with the new token's PKCS#11 configuration, and `--lineage <file>`. Measured: with v2 on and `minSdk` 29, apksigner refuses the new key alone ("v2 signing enabled but the oldest signer in the SigningCertificateLineage is missing"); with v2 off it still refuses ("The provided targeted signer configs do not cover the SDK range for V3 support; either provide the original signer or ensure a signer targets SDK version 29"); signing with both writes a v2 block and a v3.0 block (SDK 24–32) by the original key and a v3.1 block (`0x1b93ad61`, SDK 33+) by the new one. With `--rotation-min-sdk-version 28` and v2 off, the new key alone signs a v3.0 block with the lineage (Q9's alternative). V-D6 tests the two-signer PKCS#11 form with two tokens (V-D26).

`ghost-release-ops apk-core` refuses to continue unless the core of `signed.apk` equals `unsigned.apk` byte for byte (V-D5 on a software key with real apksigner output, padding p = 0 and p > 0; V-D6 on the token). v4 (`.idsig`) is only for incremental installs over ADB (source.android.com, v4) and is not produced.

### 6.6 Variation matrix and diagnostics (S0a; then on recipe changes and monthly on `main`)

Environment A builds the same commit twice per row and compares: checkout path (`/w/src` vs `/w/src2`); processor count seen by the tools (`ActiveProcessorCount`, Gradle workers and `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS` at 2 vs 8; if the output depends on it, N stays fixed at 2 and the dependence is recorded); listing order of the sources (tmpfs vs the image's overlay); date (two days apart); uid and umask; and A against B (the only row that varies the CPU model and the kernel). A difference is classified with **diffoscope** (GPL-3.0, CI diagnostic image only, never shipped) into native, dex, resources, zip metadata or other, and each class gets a measure in §6.3 before S3 closes. If an R8 difference cannot be fixed, Q-level escalation (R-D1): pin R8 by version and threads, or report upstream; the gate is never relaxed to "logically equivalent".

### 6.7 Provenance and SBOM: what exists, what this phase adds (FR-8.1, C-08)

| Control | Exists today | Phase 14 adds |
|---|---|---|
| Rust dependencies | `--locked`; `cargo-deny` (licences, bans by exact name, crates.io only) and `cargo-audit` in `rust` (`ci.yml:61-176`); the client crate allowlist on both ABIs and the crypto pins per graph (`rust-client-allowlist.sh`, `rust-crypto-pins.sh`); the vendored-source scans G-R5, G-R8 | the client graph gains only the workspace crate `ghost-client-update`, one allowlist entry (G-D6); per-target CycloneDX SBOMs in every release, with the workspace's `repository` field replaced by the project identity (D25, G-D9) |
| Gradle dependencies | dependency verification with SHA-256 for 332 components, signatures off, `.pom`/`.module` and `aapt2` trusted (`verification-metadata.xml:4-9`); the group allowlist and denylist (T7); per-coordinate licences (G-L2) on `releaseRuntimeComponents` | `aapt2` pinned (G-D8); signature verification decided (Q22); the runtime components in the SBOM |
| Toolchains | Rust 1.98.1, NDK 30.0.16248370, cargo-ndk 4.1.2, protoc 36.1 by hash, Gradle by hash; JDK 17 unpinned at patch level | one `pins.txt` for all of them, JDK by hash (G-D2) |
| Licence notices | generated and compared (G-L1), in the APK (G-L3), MPL sources (G-L4), no public binaries (G-L5) | notices hashed into each release record; MPL source bundle published on the mirrors |
| Source identity | commits unsigned (STATUS:25) | releases only from hardware-signed tags (D22); the commit bound in every manifest and attestation |
| Artifact identity | same-path T12 for the APK and the libraries | two environments, the auditor's attestation, the log (§5, §6.4) |

---

## 7. Mirrors, the update protocol and the client (normative; ADR-51)

### 7.1 Mirrors

A mirror is a v3 onion service (the relay template, `infra/relay/torrc`: `HiddenServicePoWDefensesEnabled 1`, `SafeLogging 1`, `SocksPort 0`) in front of a static file tree, **with no request logs** (ADR-11's discipline; an aggregate counter is not kept either). Each mirror has its own onion key (never in the repository; G-D1 extends `entitlement-schedule.sh`'s onion-key search to `infra/mirror`). Production needs mirrors of **at least two operators** (the mainnet root's anchor mirrors span two operator ids, §4.3); a relay operator may run one as a separate onion service. Staging runs two owner mirrors with distinct labels (X-D13, as staging relays do, `relay-directory.txt:10-13`).

**Two listeners on one onion** (revision 1 served HTTP/2 with prior knowledge only, which Tor Browser cannot use: browsers speak HTTP/2 only over TLS — Apache's HTTP/2 guide: "Most browsers will speak HTTP/2 only on https: URLs" — and Tor Browser fetches `http://…onion` over HTTP/1.1, so the first-install path of D1 and §8.1 would have failed):

- **the app's listener**, on the port the root or manifest names: HTTP/2 with prior knowledge and nothing else, serving the three path forms of §7.2;
- **the browser listener**, on port 80 of the same onion: HTTP/1.1 (`GET`, `HEAD`, single-range `Range`), serving `apk/`, `archive/`, `current.ghrb` and a static `index.html` that `ghost-release-ops bundle` generates at each publication (the current release's name, its APK link and SHA-256, the signing-certificate digests, the links to `ghost-verify`; no script, no external reference). `ghost-verify fetch` uses this listener. If V-D12/V-D22 show that the server detects the HTTP/2 preface and serves both protocols on one port, the app may use port 80 too.

Browsers form a population distinct from the app by protocol and headers (§2.4); the app's requests stay uniform among app clients. Server (Q20): **nginx** (BSD-2-Clause) if V-D12 confirms HTTP/2 with prior knowledge on a cleartext listener (its docs name the `h2c` protocol value, but do not state the mode) beside the HTTP/1.1 one, else **`ghost-mirror`** (Rust; hyper's server half already in the relay's graph; serves only the fixed paths below; no logging, gate `no-logging.sh` scope). Either way the server: listens on loopback for its Tor daemon only; serves `GET` on the path forms of §7.2 with `content-length` and single-range `Range` support, and 404 for anything else; sets no cookie and no cache-validation header; keeps no access log, error log of requests or counter; reads a publish directory updated by atomic rename (§5.2 step 5).

**No-log configuration, enforced (G-D10).** nginx logs by default, and the official image links its access and error logs to the container's stdout and stderr, which Docker's default `json-file` driver stores on disk; the relay compose file sets no logging driver, and a mirror deployment copied from it would inherit that. The committed configuration therefore sets `access_log off`, `error_log` at `crit` to stderr, `log_not_found off`, `server_tokens off`, `etag off`, `if_modified_since off`, and every compose service `logging: driver: none`; G-D10 checks these lines with negative fixtures, and a container test in S8 serves requests (found, missing, ranged, malformed) and asserts that no file appeared in the container's writable layer and no line on its stdout or stderr.

**Hosting rule** (Tor Project onion-service opsec: the most prominent attacks profile a service's availability and match induced traffic, and a mirror serves about 70 MiB to anyone on demand): never on the owner's home connection, the signer host or a CI host; one Tor instance per onion service (the SSH upload onion runs in its own Tor instance, apart from the serving one, and a co-hosted relay in yet another); C Tor's vanguards-lite (its default for onion services) at least. OA-6 and Q21 apply it. The deployment (`infra/mirror/`: torrc files, server configuration, compose file, Romanian runbook) mirrors `infra/relay/`.

**Proof of work** stays enabled, as on the relays: under an introduction flood it lets browsers that solve PoW through, while GHOST clients — which do not solve it (`infra/relay/torrc` note; `hs-pow-full` is forbidden by `rust-feature-policy.sh`) — wait in the zero-effort queue, so a flood can hold updaters until it ends or the manifest expires (DS-19, L10-D18). Q40 asks whether to enable Arti's PoW client for the `Update` scope only once it is stable.

### 7.2 Paths and HTTP rules

| Path (`<net>` = `mainnet`, `stagenet`, or `stagenet-drill` for the live drill's gate build only, D26) | Content | Client use |
|---|---|---|
| `/ghost/v1/<net>/current.ghrb` | the current bundle | every update run, from two mirrors |
| `/ghost/v1/<net>/apk/<64 hex>` | a signed APK by SHA-256 | download, `Range: bytes=<4 MiB × i>-<min(4 MiB × (i+1), apk_length) − 1>` |
| `/ghost/v1/<net>/archive/<64 hex>` | the release record (§5.3) | never by the app; `ghost-verify`, monitors, operators |
| `/` (browser listener only) | the static index page | never by the app; browsers |

Rules (the client's request builder is closed, test PM-D3; **the native side builds every path and range** from verified state — the network from the embedded root, the APK's SHA-256 and length from the offered release, the range from a chunk index it bounds by the length — and accepts no path, offset or onion from Kotlin, §9.3, PM-D9, PM-D10): HTTP/2 with prior knowledge on the onion stream (the hyper `http2` client already in `client-core/net`, `ghost/Cargo.toml:61-62`); only `:method GET`, `:scheme http`, `:authority <onion:port>`, `:path`, and `range` for APK chunks; **no** `user-agent`, `accept-encoding`, cookies, `if-none-match`, `if-modified-since` or any value that differs between clients; responses: status 200 (bundle) or 206 with a matching `content-range` (APK), `content-length` present and within the bound of §4.8; no redirects followed; body read to the bound only; 60 s per request (the RPC deadline of `ADR-19:21`). The client's HTTP/2 connection preface, SETTINGS (header table size, push disabled, initial window, max frame size, max header list size) and WINDOW_UPDATE are set explicitly rather than left to hyper's defaults, here and in the relay and issuer clients (`relay_client.rs:429-431` sets only `http2_only(true)` today, so a dependency bump would silently change the fingerprint relays see, F-D9); the golden transcript of G-D11 pins them.

### 7.3 Update runs (scheduling; `:sync`)

**The job's fate** is one pure decision (`JobFate.of`, §9.4), in this precedence: **RETURN** (the app is visible, another activity runs, sync is wiped or halted — unchanged, `JobFate.kt:34-36`), then a **quiet run** (draw 1/8, a participant installed; unchanged, `QuietRunScheduler.kt:60`), then an **update run** (draw 1/24 among the jobs that are not quiet, an updater installed), then a background **relay session**. The update draw uses the process's random key and the run index with a new domain (`RandomSources.updateRun(index)`), the T19/T23 pattern: nothing else is an input (no update state, no pending work). App visibility decides, as it already does for quiet runs, whether a job that fires runs at all (a job firing while the app is visible returns, `SyncRuntime.kt:471-475`); so the property is that the draw — which jobs are update runs — is independent of activity, and that an update run looks the same whatever the client's state (P-D1). Update runs are **not held by the payment hold** (`relayHeld()`, `SyncRuntime.kt:485-488`): the hold keeps relay sessions away from payment times (Phase 8 §19.11), and an update run has none, so in `runJob` the update fate is decided before the hold check; the hold is one of NI-U2's twin axes.

An update run has the transport READY, **no relay session, no issuer call**, and one participant: the update engine, through a lease like `ParticipantSession` (`SessionParticipant.kt:45-73`) with a new access `updateMirror` that exists only in update runs. It lasts a **fixed duration** `D_U` (Q15; default 120 s from READY) whatever the client's state: (1) it fetches `current.ghrb` from two mirrors chosen as §7.8 says (at least one root-listed) on the `Update` isolation scope (`isolation.rs:30-31`); (2) accepts per §7.4; (3) only if an offer exists and is not staged, downloads APK chunks in index order until `D_U` (resumable, `upd_staged`); otherwise it holds its lease idle until `D_U`; (4) ends. A pending offer or download therefore never changes a job's length, its share of the JobScheduler execution quota, or the timing of later relay sessions and quiet runs (revision 1 downloaded "until the lease deadline", which spent the quota and delayed later relay sessions that relays could time against the public release date).

**Rate and latency.** One job in `1 / (7/8 × 1/24)` ≈ 27.4 is an update run: at one periodic job per 15 minutes (`JobSchedulerWake.kt:37`) that is about one update run every 7 hours when every job runs. The OS lowers the job rate by App Standby bucket: Android's execution quotas are 20 minutes per rolling 60 in the Active bucket (Android 16 and later), 10 minutes per 4 hours (Working set), per 12 hours (Frequent), per 24 hours (Rare), and once a day for 10 minutes (Restricted) (developer.android.com, "Power management restrictions"). So an update run comes every few hours to a couple of days for an app in use, about every one to two weeks in the Rare bucket, and about every four weeks in Restricted; RK4's revocations and floor raises reach such clients that late (L10-D19). Phase 13 shows how old the app's release information is (`Updates.manifestIssuedDay`, §9.5) and offers the user's own check.

**A user-requested check or download** (Phase 13) runs the same steps in the foreground process, at the user's moment, on the `Update` scope, while the app is visible (declared, like `USER_ISSUER_CALL`, `SessionParticipant.kt:44-56`). Under the `runUserIssuerCall` rule it may run while a foreground relay session is listing (`SyncRuntime.kt:124-128, 782-789`), so an operator of both a relay and a mirror can line the two up and learn that this client, whose namespaces it already sees, checks or downloads now: declared (L10-D20; Q47 offers an "update hold" instead). Nothing else ever fetches from a mirror (T51); in particular nothing happens at app start (X-D1).

### 7.4 The client's decision (normative; implemented in `ghost-client-update`, called through `UpdateNative`)

Inputs: the stored facts (§9.6: the trust epoch, the stored root chain up to the trusted root, the pair and its hash, the manifest and its proof, the floor and its root version, the conflict and root-conflict flags), the embedded root chain (in the native library), the installed `versionCode` and SDK (Kotlin passes them), and the day: **trusted** (from `:sync`'s clock trust: READY in this process and `|Δwall − Δmono| ≤ 1 h`, F7:1655) or **untrusted**, in which case every time-dependent step stops with `CLOCK_UNTRUSTED` and nothing changes. σ = 3 days (F7:58) is the distance a trusted clock may still be from true time; every "not yet valid" bound below is σ + 1 day.

**At the first start of a newly installed version** (once per installed `versionCode`; the trust anchor)

0. If the installed `versionCode` differs from the stored `epoch_version_code`: let E be the newest root of the **embedded** chain and S the stored trusted root. If the stored chain contains E byte for byte, or the embedded chain contains S byte for byte, the two are **one lineage**: the trusted root becomes the newer of E and S, the stored chain extended to it if E is newer (an APK built before an RK2 the client already adopted leaves S in place; one built after it moves the client to E), and nothing else changes. Otherwise the APK carries a different lineage — a **recovery APK** (§3.5) — and the client starts a **new trust epoch**: `epoch := SHA-256(E)`, stored chain := the embedded chain, pair := `(E.version, 0)`, floor := 0, conflict and root conflict cleared, no stored manifest. Either way `epoch_version_code` := the installed `versionCode`. The embedded chain is never truncated (it is the whole committed chain from its lineage's first root, G-D4; only a bundle's `GHRC` may start later, §4.4), so a same-lineage APK always contains the stored root or is contained by it. Only an APK install, which needs `K-APK`, can start an epoch; a bundle never can. Revision 1's rule, "the anchor is the newer root by version", let a stored root vN beat a recovery root v1′ forever, and against two stolen root keys no version number could win (§3.5).

**Accept a bundle**

1. Parse `GHRB` within bounds; `network` = the app's (`WRONG_NETWORK`).
2. **Root.** Let T be the trusted root. `GHRC.first_version ≤ T.version + 1`, else `ROOT_CHAIN_BROKEN`. Entries at or below `T.version` are compared with the stored chain: one that differs and verifies by the chain rule from its stored predecessor sets the persistent `ROOT_CONFLICT` flag (§4.4); one that does not verify is ignored. Walk from `T.version + 1`, applying the chain rule with its logging clause (§4.3: each new root's own `GHRP` verified under the previous root's policy, its leaf signed by a root or release key of the previous root); stop at the first failure (`ROOT_CHAIN_BROKEN` or `ROOT_UNLOGGED`, reported, the last good root kept). R := the newest root reached. A root older than T is never adopted (no progress, never a change).
3. **Manifest.** Parse; `root_version = R.version` and `root_sha256 = SHA-256(R)` (`ROOT_MISMATCH`); the signature block meets `(R.release.keys, R.release.t)` (`BAD_SIGNATURE`); every release has the same `min_sdk` and the mirror and relay lists are canonical and sorted, with no extra mirror equal to an anchor mirror (`MALFORMED`); **floor bound**: `floor_version_code ≤ 2^31 − 1` and `floor_version_code ≤` the highest `version_code` of a flavor-1 release of this manifest whose attestation block meets `(R.attest.keys, R.attest.t)` (with `attest.t = 0`, every flavor-1 release counts), else `FLOOR_INVALID` (revision 1 accepted any floor, so one stolen release key could set 0xFFFFFFFF, above every possible `version_code`, and end every offer for good).
4. **Log.** `GHRP.message = SHA-256(GHRM)`; `leaf_key_id ∈ R.release.keys` and the leaf signature verifies; `log < |R.logs|`; the tree head verifies under that log's key; the inclusion path recomputes the root hash; at least `R.quorum` cosignatures by distinct keys in `R.witnesses` verify; no cosignature time exceeds trusted now + 4 days (`LOG_PROOF_INVALID`, `QUORUM_NOT_MET`).
5. **Time.** `issued_day ≤ today + 4` (`NOT_YET_VALID`); the manifest is **fresh** iff `today ≤ not_after_day`. An expired manifest may still be accepted as a fact (its pair, hash, floor and conflict), but it changes no directory and contributes no mirror, and the channel is `STALE` (no offer from it): two hostile mirrors replaying an old, expired manifest whose pair is above a long-offline client's cannot reinstate withdrawn relays or mirrors.
6. **Sequence.** Manifests are ordered by the pair `(root_version, manifest_seq)`, lexicographically, against the stored pair. Lower: `ROLLBACK`, no change. Equal: identical hash → `UNCHANGED`; different hash → `CONFLICT` (flag kept for this root version, no change). Higher: accept. Because a new root version starts a new sequence space, a sequence pushed to 2⁶⁴ − 1 by a stolen release key (TUF's fast-forward attack) is escaped by RK4's root update, which TUF obtains by deleting the trusted timestamp and snapshot metadata after a key rotation.
7. **Persist** in one SQLCipher transaction: the stored chain extended to R (if newer), the manifest, proof, pair and hash; the **floor** — `max(floor, floor_version_code)` when the manifest's root version equals the stored floor's root version, else the manifest's own `floor_version_code` with that root version (a new root starts a new floor scope, so RK4 can undo a fast-forwarded floor); the **conflict** flag kept while the root version is unchanged and cleared when it rises (the evidence stays in the log); and, **only if the manifest is fresh**, the directory change of §7.8. A newer root reached in step 2 is persisted even when a later step refuses the manifest: it was verified on its own, and its key removals must take effect at once — so from that moment the stored manifest, which names the older root, is **not current**: no offer, no directory change and no manifest mirror come from it, and only the new root's anchor mirrors are asked, until a manifest under R is accepted (revision 1 kept offering from the revoked key's manifest).

Two bundles in one run (two mirrors) are processed in the order of their pairs; equal pairs with different hashes set `CONFLICT`.

**Offer**

8. From the stored manifest, only if it is **current** (its root is the trusted root; else `NO_MANIFEST`): if not fresh, `STALE`, no offer. If its `min_sdk` > SDK: `SDK_UNSUPPORTED`, no offer and no APK fetch. If installed `versionCode < floor`: `UNSUPPORTED`, and the offer is the highest flavor-1 release with `version_code ≥ floor`, ignoring the rollout bucket. Else the highest flavor-1 release with `version_code > installed`, `rollout_permille > 0` and `bucket < rollout_permille`, where `bucket = ⌊1000 · BE64(HMAC-SHA256(rollout_key, "ghost/v1/rollout" ‖ apk_sha256 ‖ rollout_salt)[0..8]) / 2^64⌋` (the APK's hash in the input makes the bucket per release even if a salt were ever reused). No offer: `UP_TO_DATE`.

**Check an APK (download, staging, and again while it is streamed into the installer)**

9. **Android-equivalent parse** (Kotlin reads only the byte ranges the native side names, §9.2; it never parses the ZIP).
   (a) From the file's last 22 bytes: an end-of-central-directory record with comment length 0 at exactly `length − 22`, a single disk, no ZIP64; `cd_offset`, `cd_size` from it; `cd_offset + cd_size = length − 22` (`APK_LAYOUT`).
   (b) The 24 bytes before `cd_offset` must end with the magic `"APK Sig Block 42"` and give the block size `s`; `B := cd_offset − s − 8`; the block's leading 8-byte size field, checked when the pass reaches it, must also equal `s` (both copies, as Android checks) (`APK_LAYOUT`).
   (c) **Padding** (§6.5): `E := unsigned_length − cd_size − 22`; `0 < E ≤ B` and `B = E + ((4096 − E mod 4096) mod 4096)` (`APK_LAYOUT`).
   (d) One in-order streaming pass computes the SHA-256 and length of the whole file and the **core** `file[0, E) ‖ file[cd_offset, length)`, with the end record's offset field set to `E`; every byte of `[E, B)` must be zero (`APK_LAYOUT`).
   (e) **The signer the platform uses at this device's SDK**, as the platform selects it: the signer of the v3.1 block (`0x1b93ad61`) whose `[minSdk, maxSdk]` contains the SDK, else the v3 block's (`0xf05368c0`) whose range contains it, else the v2 block's (`0x7109871a`); its first certificate is parsed (lengths only, no signature check: the platform verifies signatures) and hashed (`APK_SIGNER` if none applies). Revision 1 read the v3 or v2 signer regardless of SDK; after a rotation targeted at API 33 that is the **old** certificate (measured, Appendix C.3), while devices on API 33+ use the v3.1 block's new one.
10. Require: length = `apk_length` (`APK_LENGTH`); full hash = `apk_sha256` (`APK_HASH`); core length and hash = `unsigned_length`, `unsigned_sha256` (`APK_CORE`); the selected certificate's digest ∈ `R.apk_signers` (`APK_SIGNER`); the attestation block meets `(R.attest.keys, R.attest.t)` over `GHRA(release)` (`ATTESTATION_QUORUM`). **What `apk_signers` means once a lineage exists:** the digests of every certificate the platform may select on a supported SDK — after an RK3 targeted at 33, the original certificate (SDK 29–32) and the new one (SDK 33+); the original stays listed while `minSdk` ≤ 32.

Because the parse finds the block, its padding and the central directory as the platform does, a file that adds anything Android would install, outside the attested core, cannot pass step 10 (crafted-APK suite from real apksigner output, §11.1; mutants MC-D15, MC-D21, MC-D26, MC-D27).

### 7.5 Anti-rollback, floor, expiry, conflict

- The platform refuses an APK with a lower `versionCode` than the installed one ("The Android system uses the versionCode value to protect against downgrades", developer.android.com, "Version your app"). The updater never offers `version_code ≤ installed`.
- Within a trust epoch, the root version and the `(root_version, manifest_seq)` pair are append-only facts with triggers (§9.6); the floor is append-only **within its root version**. A manifest can raise the floor (RK4: a vulnerable version) up to the highest attested release it lists, never lower it; only the first manifest under a newer root sets a new floor (step 7), which is how RK4 undoes a floor a stolen key pushed up. A new epoch (step 0) resets all of them.
- **`versionCode` policy**: a release counter (1, 2, 3, …) per network in a committed file read by `app/build.gradle.kts` (replacing the literal at `:14-15`); `ghost-release-ops manifest-build` refuses a `version_code` not greater than every `version_code` in the network's logged manifests, which it reads from `archive-index.txt` carried to the signer host at RK1 step 1 (a workstation that falsified the index could make the tool accept a reused counter; the monitor's rule (b) and the log catch it). Nothing depends on the build date (T12).
- **No downgrade path** (X-D4): recovering from a bad release is a new release with a higher counter; an uninstall and reinstall loses local data (the identity comes back from the mnemonic; tokens do not, `LIM` E11).
- `CONFLICT` is persistent like `SCHEDULE_CONFLICT` (`ADR-22:22`) **for the root version at which it was seen**: a Phase 13 alarm and evidence of equivocation; it does not block later, strictly newer manifests, and a newer root (RK4) clears it, so a stolen release key cannot set a permanent alarm (revision 1's flag could never be cleared). `ROOT_CONFLICT` (§4.4) lasts for the epoch.
- `STALE`: the app keeps working on its last directory; no offer; the Phase 13 copy names it after 7 days. `NO_CHANNEL`: no mirror answered in 12 consecutive update runs (a counter, not a time column); the embedded root's anchor mirrors then join the draw (§7.8).

### 7.6 Staged rollout, halt, roll-forward (the operator's runbook R-R)

- A release enters with `rollout_permille = 50`, then 250, 500, 1000 by RK6-style re-signing (C-12's 5/25/50/100). **The salt is fixed for the release's life**: it is drawn once, when the release first enters a manifest, and every re-signing keeps it (`manifest-build` refuses a changed salt for a logged `apk_sha256`; monitor rule (g)), so each stage's clients include the previous stage's — a new salt per stage would draw a fresh 25 % that leaves part of the first 5 % out. **Halt** = re-sign with 0: clients not yet updated stop being offered it; installed ones are untouched (spec §12.2 step 5).
- **The wire rule** (P-D5, X-D17, Q46). Relays observe `protocol_version` and the issuer receives a `version` field, and both see the client's HTTP/2 fingerprint; a staged release whose wire behaviour differs would make its first few percent a cohort every relay and the issuer can see, already linked to the namespaces they serve — a join key outside L1–L7, with the clients that never update a shrinking second partition. So each release carries `release_flags` bit 0 (**wire-changing**), set by `manifest-build` exactly when the release's wire transcript (artifact kind 24, G-D11: the client's HTTP/2 preface, SETTINGS and WINDOW_UPDATE and the request shape of every RPC to relays, the issuer and mirrors) differs from the previous release's. A wire-changing release either ships the change **dormant**, activating it for everyone at one ES week fixed in the code (the release itself is then wire-neutral until that week and is staged normally, its transcript taken in the dormant state), or goes 0 → 1000 without stages; `manifest-build` refuses a wire-changing release at a permille strictly between 0 and 1000. NI-U5 checks a wire-neutral release's transcript against the previous one. Timing behaviour outside the transcript is not covered (L10-D23).
- **Health signals without telemetry** (ADR-06): users' manual crash reports (FR-8.11), testers' reports, relay-visible refusals the relays already count, issuer `status.json`. The rollback threshold of C-12 is set in the runbook by Phase 17/18; this phase rehearses halt and roll-forward.
- **Roll-forward**: the previous release's source with the next counter, through the full pipeline (two environments, attestations, RK1). A schema version (`Schema.kt:115-118`: an existing version is never changed) and an accepted ES (rule 5) never go back, so the roll-forward keeps the newer schema and ES. Hence: a release that adds a migration or a new ES gets a first stage of at least 7 days at 5 % (Q30).

### 7.7 Download, staging, installation

- The offered APK is downloaded into `noBackupFilesDir/update/<sha256>.part` in 4 MiB ranges whose paths and offsets the native side builds (one row in `upd_staged`); a finished file is checked (§7.4 steps 9–10) and renamed `<sha256>.apk`; one staged file at most; a superseded or installed one is deleted. This is the update module's only file outside SQLCipher (X-D10); the APK is public data, but its presence tells AD-8 an update is pending (L10-D16).
- **Install** (UI-less, called by Phase 13): a `PackageInstaller` session for the own package, with `setRequireUserAction(USER_ACTION_REQUIRED)` on API 31+ (earlier releases always ask the user); Kotlin reads the staged file in order and, chunk by chunk, passes it to the native check (`apkUpdate`) **and** writes the same bytes to `session.openWrite`; `session.commit` is called only if `apkFinish` returns `OK`, so the bytes the platform receives are exactly the bytes checked (no check-then-use window). The platform shows its confirmation; a non-exported receiver gets the status (T15m). After the update the new process confirms its own `versionCode`, clears the staging and reports `INSTALLED`.
- Since Android 8.0 the "Install unknown apps" permission, granted by the user per app and checked with `canRequestPackageInstalls()`, governs installs from an app (developer.android.com, Android 8.0 changes); the app declares `REQUEST_INSTALL_PACKAGES` for it (the exact session behaviour on API 29, 33 and 37 is V-D13). Phase 13 owns the prompt. On Android 14+, an app whose first installer claimed update ownership triggers a user prompt when another installer updates it (source.android.com, "App ownership"); GHOST's own sessions are user-confirmed anyway.

### 7.8 The relay directory and the mirrors

- **Bootstrap** (source `config`): the committed `ghost/protocol/entitlement/relay-directory.txt` is compiled into the native library (`include_bytes!`, the ES pattern) and exposed through `EntitlementCrypto.nativeEmbeddedRelays` as validated entries (the file belongs to the entitlement protocol, beside the ES it is checked against); at first start, if `relay_directory` is empty, `:app`'s wiring inserts them. Today **no production code fills the directory** (only tests and the device world call `RelayDirectory.upsert`, F-D3), so without this step an installed app has no relay — which blocks Phase 9's two-phone gate and Phase 10's device runs too. It therefore lands as the early slice **S5a**, independent of the manifest work (F-D11).
- **From a manifest** (source `manifest`): the first **fresh, current** manifest (§7.4 steps 5 and 7) takes the directory over. In each such acceptance transaction, upsert every `GHRM.relays` entry (an existing row, bootstrap or not, becomes source `manifest`) and retire every active entry the manifest does not list (`RelayDirectory.retire`, Phase 7 semantics: retired relays keep cursors and capabilities for a later re-add, `RelayDirectory.kt:10-14`). So a relay whose operator turned hostile can be withdrawn from every client without an app release: consumers already skip retired relays (ES slots map only to active entries by exact `onion:port`, `DirectoryEsSlots.kt:31-36`).
- **Operator ids are write-once per onion.** Relay sets need at least two distinct operators (F9 §5.2: `set(ns, w)` takes slots until three relays span ≥ 2 operators), and the outbox counts distinct operator ids (`OutboxStore.kt:140,578,681`), while `DirectoryStore.upsert` overwrites `operator_id` today (`DirectoryStore.kt:63-65`). A manifest could therefore relabel operators and reshape every namespace's relay set — metadata steering, not only availability. The engine never changes the operator id of an onion it already knows (the manifest's value for that field is ignored and the acceptance reports it); `manifest-build` refuses such a manifest (mutant MC-D31). An operator change means a new onion.
- **Routine relay changes travel with the ES.** Relay sets and capabilities are keyed to ES slots (K-SCHED, compiled into the APK, week-scheduled; F9 §5.2's six-week reader union covers slot-table changes), so a manifest's routine relay work is to **add** the entry for an ES slot ahead of its weeks — inert until then — and to withdraw relays no slot of the current or next six weeks uses. `manifest-build` refuses a manifest that omits an ES slot onion of the current or next six weeks unless the run names it as an **emergency withdrawal** (an E18 move, `ADR-22:21`, recorded with a notice in the release record), and refuses a directory under which some ES week from the release week to the ES horizon has active slots spanning fewer than two operators. An emergency withdrawal changes current relay sets at each client's next update run, so for that window writers and readers may compute different sets (a three-relay set keeps two in common after one withdrawal) and the replacement relay first sees the clients that updated first (L10-D21). A stolen release key can still withdraw relays, a logged availability attack (L10-D11); it cannot add a relay any client will use without an ES slot.
- **Mirrors** (normative). Each update run asks two mirrors: the **first** drawn uniformly from the trusted root's **anchor mirrors**; the **second** drawn uniformly from the anchor mirrors and the current fresh manifest's extra mirrors, without the first, preferring another operator id. A manifest that is expired, or not current (its root is not the trusted root), contributes no mirror; on `NO_CHANNEL` the embedded root's anchor mirrors join the draw. The draw uses the run's randomness from the process key (`RandomSources.updateRun`), never client state other than the root and manifest. Revision 1 took every mirror from the newest manifest's list, so one stolen release key (1 of 2, held by one person) plus one channel to deliver its manifest — a hostile mirror, a third-party operator, or AD-12 — could list only attacker mirrors, after which the client never again saw the vendor's revoking root (and, with an unbounded floor, never another offer); not even a reinstall over the app escaped, since the stored list still chose the mirrors.

### 7.9 The ES and the manifest (F8 F6)

The ES stays compiled into the native library with no network path (ADR-22 point 5). Each release record carries `es_seq` and `es_sha256`; the auditor attests them with the build; the monitor checks append-only across logged releases (§5.3 (d)). `ghost-release-ops manifest-build` refuses a release whose ES ends before its release week + 25 (RQ-D29: a release-time rule, so T12 is untouched). **This applies to stagenet now:** a release embedding seq 1 must ship by week 2964 (Monday 2026-10-26), and any later release needs a version whose last access week is at least its release week + 25 (`protocol/entitlement/README.md:37-42`).
